How Odysseus Distrobox/Podman is implemented
The Steam Deck host provides the Distrobox environment.
odysseus-box is the Ubuntu 24.04 container used for Odysseus'
Python runtime and application dependencies.
ChromaDB is a separate Podman container named odysseuschromadb , accessed locally through port 8100. It is not the same
container as odysseus-box .
Odysseus startup and shutdown are managed by dedicated scripts,
rather than an instruction to start everything automatically at host
boot.

/home/deck/OdysseusProject/decktools/runtime/chromadb.star
ted-by-odysseus

shutdown logic must not stop ChromaDB if the marker is absent.

before the latest startup; the startup message confirmed that
ownership remained unchanged.
Inside the container, /usr/bin/distrobox-host-exec is available
for invoking host commands when necessary. Host-level Distrobox
and Podman management should otherwise be performed on the
host.

# Groq Provider Shape
Last updated: dev@28d27ee | 2026-07-17
## Scope
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 18/588
Canonical provider ID groq; OpenAI-compatible cloud transport;
detection and
request behavior in src/llm_core.py.
## Shape
Model discovery falls back to the general data[].id identity
shape. Richer
fields require a Groq-native mapped shape even when the payload
happens to
supply modalities, supported parameters, or limits. Groq transport
may accept OpenAI-style tools and streaming extensions,
but support remains per model and account.
Runtime currently exempts Groq/OpenRouter from some parameter
stripping paths;
that is transport compatibility, not a provider-wide model capability
claim.
## Fallback And Current Gaps
Exact *.groq.com preserves Groq identity. Do not infer
Llama/Gemma model
capabilities from IDs. There is no canonical rich Groq model-card
reader or
freshness policy yet.
===== OPENAI-COMPATIBLE SPECIFICATION =====
# General OpenAI-Compatible Inventory Fallback
Last updated: dev@2e2bb52 | 2026-08-16
## Scope
Canonical compatibility identity generic_openai; identity-only
reader
src/model_capability_readers/generic_openai.py; shared
envelope and identity
helpers in src/model_capability_readers/base.py.
This is not a universal OpenAI-compatible capability schema.
Transport request
and response behavior remains in src.llm_core and provider
adapters.
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 19/588
## Accepted Inventory Shape
- {"data": [...]};
- {"models": [...]}.
Within an item, the reader recovers identity from id, name, or
model.
Bare-list payloads and key/slug-only items are not supported. It
preserves
the raw item on the in-memory record, while to_dict() includes it
only when
the caller explicitly requests include_raw=True. Capability remains
unknown.
## Disabled Capability Paths
The generic reader does not inspect capability-looking fields,
including:
- type, model_type, task, and pipeline_tag;
- top-level or nested modality fields;
- capability booleans/maps/lists;
- supported_parameters;
- context, input, output, and model-length fields.
Names, descriptions, ownership, pricing, and serialized text also
never
promote capability through this reader.
## Forward Compatibility
An explicitly configured but unknown provider ID is preserved when
the generic
reader is selected. That allows endpoint-scoped stable IDs to keep
working
while every family, modality, capability, limit, and control remains
unknown.
Non-object entries are skipped; null or malformed roots return no
records.
Provider-specific headers, request extensions, and reasoning
channels must be
selected by explicit provider/endpoint adapters. They never leak
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 20/588
through this
fallback.
Compatible tool-call syntax is likewise a runtime concern rather
than catalog capability. Current parsers recover selected
Hermes/Qwen JSON bodies nested inside tool_call wrappers and
require the full Qwen bare end delimiter; GPT-OSS compatibility
can alias names that collide with its built-in tools and reverse that
alias before local dispatch. None of those repairs grants execution
authority or proves generic tool support.
## Current Gaps
- Compatible providers differ on path prefixes, null handling, tools,
streaming usage, and strict extra-field rejection.
- Bare-list and key/slug-only inventories need explicit
normalization if a
runtime consumer later requires them.
- Safe request shaping still requires explicit endpoint/provider
configuration even when identity normalization succeeds.

# OpenAI Provider Shape
Last updated: dev@e71f8ce | 2026-08-25
## Scope
Canonical provider ID openai; API dialects OpenAI Chat
Completions and
Responses; catalog reader
src/model_capability_readers/openai.py.
## Catalog Shape
GET /v1/models returns object: list with data[] model cards
containing
id, object, created, and owned_by. This is identity and availability
metadata only. It does not claim vision, tools, reasoning, modality,
task, or
context length. The record remains unknown and keeps the raw
fields.
## Request And Response Shape
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 21/588
Chat uses messages, tools[].function, tool_choice, and
choices[].message|delta; Responses uses input, flattened
tools, output
items, and typed stream events. OpenAI may support a parameter
at the platform
level while individual models differ. A later model registry or probe
must
scope that fact before it becomes canonical model capability.
## Fallback And Safety
An explicit endpoint kind selects this provider. Automatic reader
detection accepts exact openai.com or a dot-delimited subdomain
after normalizing case/trailing dots; it is a normalization hint rather
than a trust boundary. Do not parse model IDs or ownership labels.
If a proxy returns richer fields while explicitly configured as OpenAI,
the reader preserves them as raw evidence but keeps capability
unknown.
## Current Gaps
- OpenAI's Models API does not publish the per-model capability
shape needed
for automatic canonical classification.
- Runtime model-specific sampling/reasoning behavior still needs a
maintained
structured registry or endpoint probes.

def _detect_provider(url: str) -> str:
"""Detect the API provider from a configured endpoint URL.

substring, so a URL

or a
look-alike host such as ``anthropic.com.example`` — is not
misclassified.
Unknown hosts fall back to the OpenAI-compatible default,
which the
majority of providers implement.

def delete_webhook(request: Request, webhook_id: str):
_require_admin(request)
db = SessionLocal()
try:
deleted = db.query(Webhook).filter(Webhook.id ==
webhook_id).delete()
db.commit()
if not deleted:
raise HTTPException(404, "Webhook not found")
finally:

class APIKeyManager:
def __init__(self, data_dir: str):
self.data_dir = data_dir
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 43/588
self.api_keys_file = os.path.join(data_dir, "api_keys.json")
self.key_file = os.path.join(data_dir, ".key")
def get_or_create_key(self) -> bytes:
"""Get or create encryption key for API keys"""
if os.path.exists(self.key_file):
# Older versions wrote .key with the process umask (often
0o644,
# i.e. group/world-readable). Re-restrict on read so existing
# installs heal without needing the key to be regenerated.
safe_chmod(self.key_file, 0o600)
with open(self.key_file, 'rb') as f:
return f.read()
else:
key = Fernet.generate_key()
with open(self.key_file, 'wb') as f:
f.write(key)
# This key decrypts every stored provider credential, so
restrict it
# to the owner (0o600) — it must not be group/worldreadable. No-op
# on Windows (files there are ACL-restricted to the user
already).
safe_chmod(self.key_file, 0o600)
return key
def encrypt_api_key(self, api_key: str) -> str:
"""Encrypt an API key"""
if not api_key:
return ""
f = Fernet(self.get_or_create_key())
return f.encrypt(api_key.encode()).decode()
def decrypt_api_key(self, encrypted_key: str) -> str:
"""Decrypt an API key"""
if not encrypted_key:
return ""
f = Fernet(self.get_or_create_key())
return f.decrypt(encrypted_key.encode()).decode()
def _load_raw(self) -> Dict[str, str]:

"""Load the raw, still-encrypted keys dict from disk.
Tolerates a missing/corrupt/wrong-shaped file by returning {}
— the
same robustness load() relies on at startup.
"""
if not os.path.exists(self.api_keys_file):
return {}
try:
with open(self.api_keys_file, 'r', encoding="utf-8") as f:
encrypted_keys = json.load(f)
except (json.JSONDecodeError, OSError) as e:
# A corrupt/truncated api_keys.json must not crash load()
(called on
# startup via app_initializer) — treat it as no stored keys.
logger.warning("Failed to read API keys file: %s", e)
return {}
if not isinstance(encrypted_keys, dict):
# Legacy/wrong shape (e.g. a list) — .items() would raise.
Ignore it.
logger.warning("API keys file has unexpected shape (%s);
ignoring", type(encrypted_keys).__name__)
return {}
return {
str(provider): key
for provider, key in encrypted_keys.items()
if isinstance(key, str)
}
def save(self, provider: str, api_key: str):
"""Save encrypted API key to file.
Operates on the raw (still-encrypted) on-disk dict so other
providers'
keys stay encrypted. Loading via load() first would decrypt
them and
write them back as plaintext, which then fails to decrypt on the
next
load() and silently drops those providers.
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 45/588
Uses atomic write (temp file + os.replace) so a crash, disk-full,
or
mid-write error never truncates the existing keys file.
"""
keys = self._load_raw()
keys[provider] = self.encrypt_api_key(api_key)
tmp_file = self.api_keys_file + ".tmp"
try:
with open(tmp_file, 'w', encoding="utf-8") as f:
json.dump(keys, f)
f.flush()
os.fsync(f.fileno())
os.replace(tmp_file, self.api_keys_file)
except OSError:
# Clean up temp file on failure; re-raise so callers see the
error
try:
os.remove(tmp_file)
except OSError:
pass
raise
def load(self) -> Dict[str, str]:
"""Load and decrypt API keys"""
encrypted_keys = self._load_raw()
decrypted = {}
for provider, key in encrypted_keys.items():
try:
decrypted[provider] = self.decrypt_api_key(key)
except (InvalidToken, ValueError) as e:
logger.warning("Failed to decrypt API key for %s: %s",
provider, e)
return decrypted

def _resolve_tailscale_host(hostname: str) -> Optional[str]:
"""Try to resolve a hostname via 'tailscale status' if DNS fails."""

Step 4 — findings
We have confirmed an important distinction:
Groq transport already exists in Odysseus: Groq is detected by hostname, uses the
OpenAI-compatible bearer-auth flow, and has curated model IDs.
The existing credential system is separate: APIKeyManager encrypts provider keys into
data/api_keys.json , using data/.key for encryption.
Registered endpoints have their own credential field: ModelEndpoint.api_key is
resolved at runtime by resolve_endpoint_runtime() .
The container has Python 3.12.3 at /usr/bin/python3 ; the python command is not
installed. We will use the verified interpreter name rather than assume python exists.
We should not introduce a second credential mechanism until we understand how the existing
endpoint registration and key storage work. That is the next discovery step.
Step 5 — inspect endpoint storage and credential handling
This will establish whether Groq can use Odysseus's existing endpoint mechanism, how
credentials are stored, and whether there are existing local modifications we must preserve. It
does not change anything.

class ModelEndpoint(TimestampMixin, Base):
"""Admin-configured model endpoints. Models are autodiscovered via /v1/models."""
__tablename__ = "model_endpoints"
id = Column(String, primary_key=True, index=True)
name = Column(String, nullable=False) # Display label, e.g.
"Local vLLM", "OpenRouter"
base_url = Column(String, nullable=False) # Base URL, e.g.
"http://localhost:8002/v1"
api_key = Column(EncryptedText, nullable=True) # Optional
provider API key, encrypted at rest
is_enabled = Column(Boolean, default=True)
hidden_models = Column(Text, nullable=True) # JSON list of
model IDs that failed probing
cached_models = Column(Text, nullable=True) # JSON list of
last-known model IDs (avoids probe on list)
pinned_models = Column(Text, nullable=True) # JSON list of
admin-pinned model IDs (manual, may not appear in /v1/models)
model_type = Column(String, nullable=True, default="llm") #
"llm" or "image"
# auto = classify by URL; local = self-hosted server; api/proxy =
external
# OpenAI-compatible API even when reachable through a
private/tailnet IP.
endpoint_kind = Column(String, nullable=True, default="auto")
# auto = background refresh with TTL/backoff; manual/disabled
= cached-first
# only unless an explicit endpoint probe is requested.
model_refresh_mode = Column(String, nullable=True,
default="auto")
model_refresh_interval = Column(Integer, nullable=True,
default=None)
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 58/588
model_refresh_timeout = Column(Integer, nullable=True,
default=None)
# Whether models on this endpoint accept OpenAI-style function
# schemas + emit tool_calls. Auto-detected at Cookbook
auto-
# register time from --enable-auto-tool-choice in the serve
cmd;
# can be toggled per-endpoint in the UI. NULL = unknown, falls
# back to the model-name keyword heuristic in agent_loop.py.
supports_tools = Column(Boolean, nullable=True, default=None)
# JSON object: model id -> native tool schema surface
preference.
# Values: none, compact, full. Missing key = legacy automatic
behavior.
model_tool_modes = Column(Text, nullable=True)
# Per-user ownership. NULL = legacy/shared (visible to every
user) — this
# is the historical default. When non-null, the model picker only
shows
# the endpoint to that user (admins always see everything).
owner = Column(String, nullable=True, index=True)
# Optional OAuth/session-backed credential row. Used by
subscription-backed
# providers that need refresh tokens instead of a static API key.
provider_auth_id = Column(String, nullable=True, index=True)
class ProviderAuthSession(TimestampMixin, Base):
"""Encrypted OAuth/session credentials for refresh-aware model
providers."""
__tablename__ = "provider_auth_sessions"
id = Column(String, primary_key=True, index=True)
provider = Column(String, nullable=False, index=True)
owner = Column(String, nullable=True, index=True)
label = Column(String, nullable=True)
base_url = Column(String, nullable=False)
access_token = Column(EncryptedText, nullable=True)
refresh_token = Column(EncryptedText, nullable=True)
last_refresh = Column(DateTime, nullable=True)
auth_mode = Column(String, nullable=True)
class McpServer(TimestampMixin, Base):
"""Admin-configured MCP (Model Context Protocol) tool
servers."""

"a"/"b", "right": "a"/"b"}
voted_at = Column(DateTime, nullable=True)

Step 5 — important findings
The working tree confirms three local items that must be preserved:
src/builtin_mcp.py — modified.
decktools/ — untracked.
requirements-odysseus.txt — untracked.
I will not overwrite, reset, or clean any of them.
The more important architectural finding is that Odysseus already has encrypted API-key
storage for model endpoints: ModelEndpoint.api_key uses the database's EncryptedText
type. Its normal endpoint resolver retrieves that key for requests. This is distinct from
APIKeyManager , which is used for other saved provider secrets such as webhook credentials.
That makes the existing endpoint system the likely integration path for Groq. We should
inspect its encryption implementation and endpoint-management routes before deciding
anything about the separate host-side key-file design.
Step 6 — inspect endpoint encryption and management
STEAM DECK — HOST
Read-only. This checks how endpoint keys are encrypted, added, updated, and tested. It does
not reveal stored key values.

from src.endpoint_resolver import resolve_url

host-local

Step 6 — conclusion
The existing Groq integration is more complete than a simple hostname mapping:
Groq has dedicated provider detection and URL-building tests.
Its model curation and invalid-key probe behaviour already have tests.
The normal endpoint registration flow accepts an API key and stores it in
ModelEndpoint.api_key .
The endpoint update route supports changing or clearing that key without deleting and
recreating the endpoint.
The database column is explicitly marked as encrypted at rest.
This strongly suggests we should integrate Groq through Odysseus's existing Model
Endpoint system, not build a parallel Groq key manager. That also avoids introducing another
credential store without a demonstrated need.
One important detail remains: we need to verify exactly how EncryptedText encrypts and
decrypts the database value, and inspect the actual endpoint probe route. The earlier probe
excerpt did not include the function itself, so I won't infer its behaviour from that partial output.
Step 7 — verify encryption and Groq connection testing

"""Text column transparently encrypted at rest via
src.secret_storage.

plaintext, so all consumers use the column normally. Legacy
plaintext

migration
encrypts them). Protects the SQLite file at rest (stolen backup /

===== 1. SECRET STORAGE IMPLEMENTATION =====
"""
secret_storage.py
Fernet-based symmetric encryption for secrets stored in the SQLite
DB
(IMAP / SMTP passwords today; safe to extend). The key lives at
data/.app_key, mode 0o600, generated on first call. data/ is
gitignored so the key never ships with the repo.
Threat model: protects against SQLite-file exfiltration (stolen
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 91/588
backup, leaked container layer, sibling-tenant read). Does **not**
protect against a process compromise — anyone who can read this
module's memory or the key file has plaintext.
Encrypted values carry an enc: prefix so the migration is
idempotent: passing an already-encrypted value to encrypt() is a
no-op; passing a plaintext value to decrypt() returns it
unchanged. That lets legacy rows coexist with new ones until a
single migration pass rewrites them.
"""
import os
import logging
from pathlib import Path
from cryptography.fernet import Fernet, InvalidToken
from core.platform_compat import safe_chmod
from src.constants import APP_KEY_FILE
logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)
_KEY_PATH = Path(APP_KEY_FILE)
_PREFIX = "enc:"
_fernet: Fernet | None = None
def _load_or_create_key() -> bytes:
if _KEY_PATH.exists():
return _KEY_PATH.read_bytes()
_KEY_PATH.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
key = Fernet.generate_key()
_KEY_PATH.write_bytes(key)
# POSIX: lock the key to 0o600. Windows: no-op (the user-profile
data dir is
# already ACL-restricted); safe_chmod swallows both cases.
safe_chmod(_KEY_PATH, 0o600)
logger.info(f"Generated new app key at {_KEY_PATH}")
return key
def _get_fernet() -> Fernet:
global _fernet
if _fernet is None:
_fernet = Fernet(_load_or_create_key())
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 92/588
return _fernet
def encrypt(plaintext: str) -> str:
"""Encrypt a string. Empty input passes through. Alreadyencrypted
values pass through unchanged so re-encrypting is a no-op."""
if not plaintext:
return plaintext or ""
if plaintext.startswith(_PREFIX):
return plaintext
token = _get_fernet().encrypt(plaintext.encode("utf8")).decode("ascii")
return _PREFIX + token
def decrypt(value: str) -> str:
"""Decrypt an enc:-prefixed value. Plaintext (legacy) passes
through unchanged. Returns "" on decryption failure so a corrupt
or rotated-key row degrades to "unconfigured" rather than
500."""
if not value:
return value or ""
if not value.startswith(_PREFIX):
return value
try:
return
_get_fernet().decrypt(value[len(_PREFIX):].encode("ascii")).decode("u
tf-8")
except InvalidToken:
logger.error("Failed to decrypt stored secret — wrong key or
corrupt token")
return ""
except Exception as e:
logger.error(f"Decrypt failure: {e}")
return ""
def is_encrypted(value: str) -> bool:
return bool(value) and value.startswith(_PREFIX)

_redact_url_for_log(url))

else "unknown"
logger.warning("Failed to probe %s with API key: HTTP %s",
_redact_url_for_log(url), status)
return []
logger.warning("Failed to probe %s: %s",
_redact_url_for_log(url), e)
except Exception as e:
if api_key:
logger.warning("Failed to probe %s with API key: %s",
_redact_url_for_log(url), e)
return []

def test_model_endpoint(

outbound
# request. Local-first means loopback/LAN endpoints are
allowed by

are always

for full lockdown.

base_url,
block_private=os.getenv("MODELENDPOINT_BLOCK_PRIVATE_IPS",
"false").lower() == "true",
)
if not ok:
raise HTTPException(400, f"Rejected endpoint URL:
{reason}")

_safe_detect_provider(base_url) == "featherless"
if is_featherless and requested_kind == "auto":
requested_kind = "api"
configured_timeout =
_parse_positive_int(model_refresh_timeout, minimum=1,
maximum=60)
probe_timeout = _explicit_model_list_timeout(base_url,
requested_kind, configured_timeout)
models = _probe_endpoint(base_url, api_key.strip() or None,
timeout=probe_timeout)
ping = {"reachable": True, "error": None} if models else

Step 8 — findings
We have confirmed an important security detail:
Odysseus encrypts endpoint API keys at rest using Fernet.
The encryption key is stored in data/.app_key , generated on first use, with file
permissions set to 0600 .
ModelEndpoint.api_key uses the encrypted database column.
Groq already has a model-list probe path using the OpenAI-compatible /models
endpoint.
A keyed probe that receives an authentication failure returns no models; it does not
silently substitute the curated list.
Design implication: we should not introduce a second Groq credential mechanism until we
understand the existing endpoint lifecycle. The existing encrypted endpoint-key mechanism
may already provide most of what Groq needs. We still need to verify how keys are resolved,
how a connection test reports authentication failures, and how this fits the separate host-side
key-storage decision.
No files or settings have been changed.## Step 9 — trace the existing credential path
Next, let's establish exactly where Odysseus stores its encryption key and how endpoint probes
retrieve a decrypted provider key. This is read-only; the command does not display any key
contents.

"""Admin-configured model endpoints. Models are autodiscovered via /v1/models."""

"Local vLLM", "OpenRouter"
base_url = Column(String, nullable=False) # Base URL, e.g.
"http://localhost:8002/v1"
api_key = Column(EncryptedText, nullable=True) # Optional
provider API key, encrypted at rest
is_enabled = Column(Boolean, default=True)
hidden_models = Column(Text, nullable=True) # JSON list of
model IDs that failed probing
cached_models = Column(Text, nullable=True) # JSON list of
last-known model IDs (avoids probe on list)
pinned_models = Column(Text, nullable=True) # JSON list of
admin-pinned model IDs (manual, may not appear in /v1/models)
model_type = Column(String, nullable=True, default="llm") #
"llm" or "image"
# auto = classify by URL; local = self-hosted server; api/proxy =
external
# OpenAI-compatible API even when reachable through a
private/tailnet IP.
endpoint_kind = Column(String, nullable=True, default="auto")
# auto = background refresh with TTL/backoff; manual/disabled
= cached-first
# only unless an explicit endpoint probe is requested.
model_refresh_mode = Column(String, nullable=True,
default="auto")
model_refresh_interval = Column(Integer, nullable=True,
default=None)
model_refresh_timeout = Column(Integer, nullable=True,
default=None)

===== 4. API KEY MANAGER =====
import os
import json
import logging
from typing import Dict
from cryptography.fernet import Fernet, InvalidToken
from core.platform_compat import safe_chmod
logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)
class APIKeyManager:
def __init__(self, data_dir: str):
self.data_dir = data_dir
self.api_keys_file = os.path.join(data_dir, "api_keys.json")
self.key_file = os.path.join(data_dir, ".key")
def get_or_create_key(self) -> bytes:
"""Get or create encryption key for API keys"""
if os.path.exists(self.key_file):
# Older versions wrote .key with the process umask (often
0o644,
# i.e. group/world-readable). Re-restrict on read so existing
# installs heal without needing the key to be regenerated.
safe_chmod(self.key_file, 0o600)
with open(self.key_file, 'rb') as f:
return f.read()
else:
key = Fernet.generate_key()
with open(self.key_file, 'wb') as f:
f.write(key)
# This key decrypts every stored provider credential, so
restrict it
# to the owner (0o600) — it must not be group/worldreadable. No-op
# on Windows (files there are ACL-restricted to the user
already).
safe_chmod(self.key_file, 0o600)
return key
def encrypt_api_key(self, api_key: str) -> str:
"""Encrypt an API key"""
if not api_key:
return ""
f = Fernet(self.get_or_create_key())
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 110/588
return f.encrypt(api_key.encode()).decode()
def decrypt_api_key(self, encrypted_key: str) -> str:
"""Decrypt an API key"""
if not encrypted_key:
return ""
f = Fernet(self.get_or_create_key())
return f.decrypt(encrypted_key.encode()).decode()
def _load_raw(self) -> Dict[str, str]:
"""Load the raw, still-encrypted keys dict from disk.
Tolerates a missing/corrupt/wrong-shaped file by returning {}
— the
same robustness load() relies on at startup.
"""
if not os.path.exists(self.api_keys_file):
return {}
try:
with open(self.api_keys_file, 'r', encoding="utf-8") as f:
encrypted_keys = json.load(f)
except (json.JSONDecodeError, OSError) as e:
# A corrupt/truncated api_keys.json must not crash load()
(called on
# startup via app_initializer) — treat it as no stored keys.
logger.warning("Failed to read API keys file: %s", e)
return {}
if not isinstance(encrypted_keys, dict):
# Legacy/wrong shape (e.g. a list) — .items() would raise.
Ignore it.
logger.warning("API keys file has unexpected shape (%s);
ignoring", type(encrypted_keys).__name__)
return {}
return {
str(provider): key
for provider, key in encrypted_keys.items()
if isinstance(key, str)
}
def save(self, provider: str, api_key: str):
"""Save encrypted API key to file.
Operates on the raw (still-encrypted) on-disk dict so other
providers'
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 111/588
keys stay encrypted. Loading via load() first would decrypt
them and
write them back as plaintext, which then fails to decrypt on the
next
load() and silently drops those providers.
Uses atomic write (temp file + os.replace) so a crash, disk-full,
or
mid-write error never truncates the existing keys file.
"""
keys = self._load_raw()
keys[provider] = self.encrypt_api_key(api_key)
tmp_file = self.api_keys_file + ".tmp"
try:
with open(tmp_file, 'w', encoding="utf-8") as f:
json.dump(keys, f)
f.flush()
os.fsync(f.fileno())
os.replace(tmp_file, self.api_keys_file)
except OSError:
# Clean up temp file on failure; re-raise so callers see the
error
try:
os.remove(tmp_file)
except OSError:
pass
raise
def load(self) -> Dict[str, str]:
"""Load and decrypt API keys"""
encrypted_keys = self._load_raw()
decrypted = {}
for provider, key in encrypted_keys.items():
try:
decrypted[provider] = self.decrypt_api_key(key)
except (InvalidToken, ValueError) as e:
logger.warning("Failed to decrypt API key for %s: %s",
provider, e)
return decrypted

Step 9 — findings
The existing endpoint credential path is now clear:
ModelEndpoint.api_key is encrypted using src.secret_storage .
Its encryption key is APP_KEY_FILE , which resolves to DATA_DIR/.app_key .
Endpoint probes retrieve the decrypted key through resolve_endpoint_runtime() .
The separate APIKeyManager has its own .key and api_keys.json store. It is a different
credential system and should not be confused with endpoint credentials.
The proposed host-side Groq key directory does not currently exist.
My recommendation: avoid creating a third credential store. First establish where Odysseus's
actual DATA_DIR resides and whether the container can access its .app_key reliably. If the
existing endpoint system meets the security and operational requirements, Groq can use it
without a parallel key-file mechanism.

the API/UI

the running

def _load_build_version() -> str:
override = os.getenv("ODYSSEUS_BUILD_VERSION", "").strip()
if override:
return override

encoding="utf-8") as fh:

if value:
return value
except OSError:
pass
return "unknown"

📦[deck@odysseus-box ~/OdysseusProject]$
Excellent. Step 16 establishes exactly when the encryption key is generated.
What the source code proves
1. No key is generated just by importing secret_storage .
2. _load_or_create_key() creates .app_key only when encryption or decryption actually
needs Fernet.
3. Writing a non-empty, unencrypted API key into ModelEndpoint.api_key invokes
encrypt() and creates .app_key if missing.
4. Reading a NULL API-key column does not create the key.
5. The key is restricted to permission 0600 .
6. The database column encrypts credentials at rest, but the key and database currently
reside in the same data directory.
That last point matters: the built-in mechanism protects the database file by itself, but it does
not provide separation between the encrypted database and its encryption key.
My recommendation
Keep our original credential-storage requirement alive:
/home/deck/.config/odysseus/providers/groq/odysseus-groq.key
I would not yet create that file or introduce a second credential mechanism. First, let's
determine whether the existing endpoint registration system can accommodate our security
requirement without unnecessary modifications.

4. Odysseus WebUI customization
Yes, customization is something we should investigate.
I propose three levels:
1. Settings configuration: use existing options wherever possible.
2. Custom dashboard/menu: add provider status, diagnostics and
useful shortcuts.
3. Custom HTML/CSS/JS: only if necessary, preferably through
isolated files rather than modifying core application logic.
We must first inspect how the WebUI is structured. I will not
promise that arbitrary HTML changes are safe until we understand
its frontend architecture.
Any customization should be reversible, backed up and tested after
application updates.

async def probe_local_endpoints(request: Request):
"""Fast parallel reachability check for LOCAL endpoints only.
Cloud endpoints (api.openai.com, api.anthropic.com, etc.) are
assumed up. Local endpoints get a 1.5s cheap reachability
probe so the UI
can dim stale entries pointing at dead vLLM servers. Returns
{ep_id: {alive, latency_ms, error}}."""
require_admin(request)
now = _time.time()
if (_local_probe_cache["data"] is not None and
(now - _local_probe_cache["time"]) <
_LOCAL_PROBE_TTL):
return _local_probe_cache["data"]
import asyncio as _asyncio
task = _local_probe_inflight.get("task")
if task is not None and not task.done():
return await task
async def _compute_local_probe() -> Dict[str, Any]:

if _disable_stale_cookbook_local_endpoints(db):
_invalidate_models_cache()
endpoints =

local_eps.append((ep.id, base, ep.api_key))
finally:
db.close()

key = _refresh_key(base, api_key)
grouped.setdefault(key, {"base": base, "api_key": api_key,

async def _probe_one(data: Dict[str, Any]) -> Dict[str, Any]:
t0 = _time.time()
try:
# Bumped 1.5s → 3.5s. The previous 1.5s budget was
clipping
# local vLLM endpoints on Tailscale links where the
model
# server is still loading (Qwen3.5-122B takes 2–3 min to
# warm); /v1/models can take 500–2500 ms on a busy
box,
# which pushed _ping_endpoint's full path-discovery
sweep
# past the cap and marked the row offline despite the
# user actively chatting with it.
ping = await _asyncio.to_thread(_ping_endpoint,
data["base"], data.get("api_key"), 3.5)
lat = round((_time.time() - t0) * 1000)
return {
"alive": bool(ping.get("reachable")),
"latency_ms": lat,
"status_code": ping.get("status_code"),
"error": ping.get("error"),
}
except Exception as e:
return {"alive": False, "latency_ms": None, "status_code":
None, "error": str(e)[:120]}
results_list = await _asyncio.gather(
*[_probe_one(data) for data in grouped.values()],
return_exceptions=False,
)
results: Dict[str, Any] = {}
for data, r in zip(grouped.values(), results_list):

for eid in data["endpoint_ids"]:
results[eid] = r
_local_probe_cache["data"] = results
_local_probe_cache["time"] = _time.time()
return results
task = _asyncio.create_task(_compute_local_probe())
_local_probe_inflight["task"] = task
try:
return await task
finally:
if _local_probe_inflight.get("task") is task:
_local_probe_inflight["task"] = None
@router.get("/ping")
def ping_endpoints(request: Request):
"""Probe all enabled endpoints and return status + latency."""
require_admin(request)
db = SessionLocal()
try:
endpoints =
db.query(ModelEndpoint).filter(ModelEndpoint.is_enabled ==
True).all()
finally:
db.close()
results = []
for ep in endpoints:

kind = _effective_endpoint_kind(ep, base)
cached_count = len(_cached_model_ids(ep))
entry = {
"id": ep.id,
"name": ep.name,
"base_url": base,
"provider": provider,
"category": _classify_endpoint(base, kind),
"endpoint_kind": kind,
}
try:
t0 = _time.time()
ping = _ping_endpoint(base, ep.api_key, timeout=1.5)
entry["latency_ms"] = round((_time.time() - t0) * 1000)

https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 167/588

("online" if ping.get("reachable") or cached_count else "offline")
entry["error"] = ping.get("error")
entry["model_count"] = cached_count or
(len(ANTHROPIC_MODELS) if provider == "anthropic" else 0)
except Exception as e:
entry["latency_ms"] = None
entry["status"] = "online" if cached_count else "offline"

Worked for 17s
Step 20 identifies two concrete credential bypasses:
/model-endpoints/ping sends ep.api_key directly to the health probe.
/model-endpoints/local-probe also reads the database field directly.
The endpoint-listing route only reports whether a database key exists; it does not currently
account for a key stored in an external file.
This means our provider integration must make runtime authentication, connection testing,
and status reporting agree on the same credential source. Otherwise Groq could work in chat
but appear unauthenticated in the dashboard.

def _gather_inputs() -> Dict[str, Any]:
"""Pull live config/account/endpoint lists from the app's data
sources.
Each lookup fails soft: a broken source yields an empty/neutral
value so a
single failure can't take down the whole health report.
"""
settings: Dict[str, Any] = {}
integrations: List[Dict[str, Any]] = []
accounts: List[Dict[str, Any]] = []
endpoints: List[Dict[str, Any]] = []
try:
from src.settings import load_settings
settings = load_settings() or {}
except Exception as e:
logger.debug(f"service_health: settings load failed: {e}")
try:
from src.integrations import load_integrations
integrations = load_integrations() or []
except Exception as e:
logger.debug(f"service_health: integrations load failed: {e}")
try:
from routes.email_helpers import _list_email_accounts
accounts = _list_email_accounts() or []
except Exception as e:
logger.debug(f"service_health: email accounts load failed: {e}")
try:
from core.database import SessionLocal, ModelEndpoint
db = SessionLocal()
try:
rows = db.query(ModelEndpoint).filter(
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 175/588
ModelEndpoint.is_enabled == True).all() # noqa: E712
endpoints = [{"name": r.name, "base_url": r.base_url,
"api_key": r.api_key} for r in rows]
finally:
db.close()
except Exception as e:
logger.debug(f"service_health: endpoint load failed: {e}")
return {"settings": settings, "integrations": integrations,
"accounts": accounts, "endpoints": endpoints}
async def _run_subsystem(name: str, fn: Callable, *args: Any) ->
Dict[str, Any]:
"""Run one (sync) subsystem probe in a thread under a hard
deadline.
A subsystem that overruns _SUBSYSTEM_DEADLINE (or raises)
becomes a
controlled down/timeout entry instead of hanging or leaking the
error.
"""
try:
return await asyncio.wait_for(asyncio.to_thread(fn, *args),
timeout=_SUBSYSTEM_DEADLINE)
except asyncio.TimeoutError:
return _svc(name, DOWN, _detail_for("timeout"),
error="timeout")
except Exception as e:
category = _classify_error(e)
return _svc(name, DOWN, _detail_for(category),
error=category)

Step 22 shows that the session and chat routes have a clear path toward consistent credential
handling:
Session creation currently copies the database key into validation headers and saved
session headers.
Session switching repeats that direct lookup.
Compare and chat route switching also build headers directly from ep.api_key .
The webhook fallback already uses resolve_endpoint_runtime() for OAuth endpoints,
but only resolves static credentials directly from the database.
These are good candidates for using the shared resolver. We must preserve explicit per-request
API keys where the application allows them, and preserve owner checks and OAuth behavior.
We should not blindly replace session headers or change the authentication model.

# ── Email (IMAP) ──
def email_health(accounts: List[Dict[str, Any]],
*, connect: Optional[Callable] = None) -> Dict[str, Any]:
"""Try a short IMAP connect+logout per configured account,
concurrently.
All connect → ok. Some fail → degraded. All fail → down. No
account
configured → disabled. Bounded by _FANOUT_BUDGET regardless
of count.
meta carries only the account label and a controlled error
category —
never credentials or raw exception text.
"""
if not accounts:
return _svc("email", DISABLED, "No email accounts
configured.")
if connect is None:
from routes.email_helpers import _imap_connect
# Impose the service-health budget on the IMAP connect
itself.
connect = lambda aid: _imap_connect(aid,
timeout=_PROBE_TIMEOUT) # noqa: E731
def _label(acc: Dict[str, Any]) -> str:
return acc.get("account_name") or acc.get("account_id") or
"account"
def _check(_i: int, acc: Dict[str, Any]) -> Dict[str, Any]:
name = _label(acc)
if not (acc.get("imap_host") or ""):
return {"name": name, "ok": False, "error": "no_host"}
try:
conn = connect(acc.get("account_id"))
try:
conn.logout()
except Exception:
pass
return {"name": name, "ok": True, "error": None}
except Exception as e:
return {"name": name, "ok": False, "error": _classify_error(e)}
raw = _bounded_map(accounts, _check,
budget=_FANOUT_BUDGET,
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 203/588
concurrency=_PROBE_CONCURRENCY)
per_account = [r if r is not None
else {"name": _label(accounts[i]), "ok": False, "error":
"timeout"}
for i, r in enumerate(raw)]
return _rollup_items("email", "mailbox(es)", per_account)
# ── Provider endpoints ──
def providers_health(endpoints: List[Dict[str, Any]],
*, probe: Optional[Callable] = None) -> Dict[str, Any]:
"""Probe each enabled model endpoint's model list, concurrently.
endpoints is a list of plain dicts ({name, base_url, api_key}) so
this
stays decoupled from the ORM and trivially testable. Non-empty
model list
→ reachable. Bounded by _FANOUT_BUDGET regardless of count.
meta never
contains api_key or raw URLs — only a display name (or a
sanitized URL when
no name is set) and a controlled error category.
"""
if not endpoints:
return _svc("providers", DISABLED, "No model endpoints
configured.")
if probe is None:
from routes.model_routes import _probe_endpoint as probe
def _label(ep: Dict[str, Any]) -> str:
return ep.get("name") or _safe_url(ep.get("base_url")) or
"endpoint"
def _check(_i: int, ep: Dict[str, Any]) -> Dict[str, Any]:
name = _label(ep)
try:
models = probe(ep.get("base_url"), ep.get("api_key"),
timeout=_PROBE_TIMEOUT) or []
except Exception as e:
return {"name": name, "ok": False, "model_count": 0,
"error": _classify_error(e)}
count = len(models)
return {"name": name, "ok": bool(count), "model_count": count,
"error": None if count else "no_models"}

Worked for 14s
Step 24 confirms that service health uses the same basic model as endpoint discovery: it
probes a URL with an API key and considers a non-empty model list reachable. Its error
reporting is deliberately sanitized.
That gives us a sensible shared design:
1. One provider credential resolver reads the relevant host-mounted key file.
2. The existing endpoint resolver uses it for supported static-key providers.
3. Health checks and endpoint tests use the same resolved credential.
4. The database remains free of copies of those provider keys.
5. OAuth/session-backed and local endpoints retain their existing authentication paths.
We should also distinguish key-file presence from provider connectivity: a file existing does
not mean the key is valid. The connection test must verify authentication and report an auth
failure separately from a network failure.

Steps 20–25 — findings
The important architectural conclusion is now clear: host-file credentials cannot be added
safely by changing only endpoint_resolver.py .
Odysseus reads ModelEndpoint.api_key directly in several independent paths:
Session creation and endpoint switching.
Chat endpoint selection.
Compare sessions.
Provider health checks and endpoint ping.
Webhook fallback.
Gallery image operations.
Endpoint probing and key updates.
If Groq's key lives exclusively in the host file, these paths must obtain it consistently without
copying it into the database. Otherwise, some features may work while others silently fail.
The container identity is encouraging: the project runs as UID 1000 ( deck ), matching the host
file owner's UID. A host key file with owner-only permissions ( 600 ) should therefore be
readable by the application process if the relevant host directory is mounted into the
container and its permissions allow traversal. We have not yet verified the provider directory
itself.
I recommend we keep the seven-provider host-file layout as the intended credential source
and inspect the existing tests before designing the smallest safe integration. No files have been
changed.

10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 212/588
One correction to our earlier interpretation: podman inspect reports the container's
configured user as root:root , but the running Distrobox session and project Python execute
as UID 1000 ( deck ). For key-file access, the effective runtime identity is the relevant one.
There is one security distinction still to settle: the home mount is read-write. Mode 600
protects the key from other users, but it does not prevent the Odysseus process running as
deck from modifying its own key file. We should inspect the Distrobox configuration before
deciding whether a read-only submount is practical.

153-def _bounded_map(items: List[Any], worker: Callable[[int, Any],
Dict[str, Any]],
154- *, budget: float = _FANOUT_BUDGET,
155- concurrency: int = _PROBE_CONCURRENCY) ->
List[Optional[Dict[str, Any]]]:
156- """Run ``worker(index, item)`` across a bounded thread pool,
in order.
157-
158- worker must catch its own exceptions and return a per-item
dict. Any item
159- not finished within budget seconds *in total* is left as
``None`` (the
160- caller substitutes a controlled timeout entry). The pool is
shut down with
161- ``wait=False`` so stragglers never block the response — their
own per-op
162- timeout reaps them shortly after.
163- """
164- n = len(items)
165- out: List[Optional[Dict[str, Any]]] = [None] * n
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 226/588
ChatGPT said:
166- if n == 0:
167- return out
168- ex =
concurrent.futures.ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=max(1,
min(concurrency, n)))
169- futures = {ex.submit(worker, i, items[i]): i for i in range(n)}
170- try:
171- for fut in concurrent.futures.as_completed(futures,
timeout=budget):
172- i = futures[fut]
173- try:
174- out[i] = fut.result()
175- except Exception as e: # worker is expected to handle
its own errors
176- out[i] = {"ok": False, "error": _classify_error(e)}
177- except concurrent.futures.TimeoutError:
178- pass # unfinished items stay None → marked timeout by
the caller
179- finally:
180- ex.shutdown(wait=False, cancel_futures=True)
181- return out

Step 29 — an important issue found
The existing probe code has a weakness we need to address before calling it a reliable provider
health test.
_probe_endpoint() returns an empty model list for many failures, including authentication
failures. In some cases it also falls back to a curated model list when discovery fails. That means:
A reachable provider with an invalid key can appear to have no models.
A provider whose discovery endpoint fails can still appear to have models through fallback.
providers_health() currently interprets a non-empty list as success.
The existing _ping_endpoint() is more informative: it can identify HTTP 401/403 responses,
but its result is not yet a unified provider status.

===== 3. HEALTH STATUS CONSTANTS =====
"""Consolidated service health / degraded-state reporting.
ROADMAP: "Better degraded-state reporting for ChromaDB,
SearXNG, email, ntfy,
and provider probes." There was no single readout of which
subsystems are
actually working — /api/health is only a liveness ping and each
subsystem's
signal lives in a different module. This collects them into one
uniform,
*non-intrusive* report (no test push is sent, no real search is run), so
the
admin endpoint built on top of it is safe to poll.
Each probe returns:
{"name": str, "status": "ok"|"degraded"|"down"|"disabled",
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 237/588
"detail": str, "meta": dict}
- ok — reachable / working
- degraded — partially working (one of several components down)
- down — configured & enabled but unreachable / erroring
- disabled — not configured or turned off (not counted as a failure)
Design notes (driven by review feedback):
- **Bounded wall-clock.** Per-item probes (providers, email
accounts) fan out
across a bounded thread pool with a hard total budget
(_FANOUT_BUDGET);
stragglers are reported as a controlled timeout rather than
blocking. The
aggregate adds a per-subsystem deadline
(_SUBSYSTEM_DEADLINE) and an overall
ceiling (_AGGREGATE_DEADLINE), so the endpoint cannot hang
regardless of how
many endpoints/accounts are configured or how slowly they
respond.
- **No secret leakage.** Even though the endpoint is admin-only,
the response
never returns credential-bearing URLs or raw exception text: URLs
are passed
through _safe_url (userinfo / query / fragment stripped) and
failures are
mapped to controlled categories via _classify_error.
The probe functions take their inputs as parameters (settings dict,
account
list, endpoint list, manager objects) and isolate the network call to
``_http_get`` / injected callables, so they unit-test without touching
the
network.
"""
import asyncio
import concurrent.futures
import logging
import socket
import ssl
import time
from typing import Any, Callable, Dict, List, Optional
from urllib.parse import urlparse
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection

logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)
# Status ordering for rolling up an overall verdict. "disabled" is
excluded —
# a turned-off feature must never drag the overall status down.
_SEVERITY = {"ok": 0, "degraded": 1, "down": 2}
OK = "ok"
DEGRADED = "degraded"
DOWN = "down"
DISABLED = "disabled"
# Timing budgets (seconds). _PROBE_TIMEOUT bounds a single
network op;
# _FANOUT_BUDGET bounds a whole fan-out (providers/email)
regardless of count;
# the aggregate layer adds a per-subsystem deadline and an overall
ceiling.
_PROBE_TIMEOUT = 4
_PROBE_CONCURRENCY = 8
_FANOUT_BUDGET = 8
_SUBSYSTEM_DEADLINE = 10
_AGGREGATE_DEADLINE = 14

def _safe_url(url: Optional[str]) -> str:
"""Strip credentials (userinfo), query, and fragment from a URL.
Keeps scheme / host / port / path so the report is still useful, but
never
echoes user:pass@, ?api_key=…, or #… back to the caller.
Returns
"<redacted>" if the URL can't be parsed into at least a host.
"""
if not url:
return ""
raw = url.strip()
try:
p = urlparse(raw if "://" in raw else "//" + raw)
host = p.hostname or ""
if not host:
return "<redacted>"
netloc = f"{host}:{p.port}" if p.port else host
path = (p.path or "").rstrip("/")
scheme = f"{p.scheme}://" if p.scheme else ""
return f"{scheme}{netloc}{path}"
except Exception:
return "<redacted>"

Step 30 — what the current tests tell us
There is already meaningful regression coverage for ping behavior, including:
HTTP 2xx success.

10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 240/588
Two details need care:
HTTP 429 means the service answered but is rate-limiting requests. It should not be
labelled OFFLINE or AUTH ERROR.
HTTP 5xx means the provider responded but is experiencing a server-side problem. That
should be distinguished from a network failure.
The existing service-health vocabulary ( ok , degraded , down , disabled ) is also separate from
the richer provider statuses we want in the dashboard. We should avoid changing general
service-health semantics just to accommodate cloud providers.

📦[deck@odysseus-box ~/OdysseusProject]$
Step 31 — findings
The endpoint test route confirms an important gap in the current design:
It accepts an API key directly from the form and passes it to _probe_endpoint() and
_ping_endpoint() .
Its response is limited to online , status , ping_error , model list and count.
A reachable server returning 401 or 403 is currently reported as offline , rather than the
distinct AUTH ERROR state required by your provider design.
The existing ping tests already verify that 401 is a server response, not a network outage.
That gives us a sound starting point for improving classification without disturbing the
existing probe behavior.
No code has been changed. We should not put a real Groq key into this form: the route is
designed around a submitted key, not yet the host-file credential source we are planning.

📦[deck@odysseus-box ~/OdysseusProject]$
Step 32 — findings
The admin UI uses one shared renderer, _renderEndpointTestResult() , for both cloud and
local endpoint tests. That is useful: we can improve the response contract centrally without
creating a second connection-test interface.
The cloud test currently sends the key as a form field. The local test does the same when a key
is supplied. We should preserve that behavior for ordinary manually configured endpoints
while adding a separate, secure credential-resolution path for the seven supported cloud
providers.
One remaining detail: the output shows the renderer's ending, but not its complete definition. I
want to inspect that and the endpoint-list status display before proposing the change.### 🔎
STEP 33 — inspect the shared result renderer and endpoint-list display

function shouldDisplayEndpointBaseUrl(ep) {
if (!ep || !ep.base_url) return false;
if (isChatgptSubscriptionEndpoint(ep)) return false;
if (isFeatherlessEndpoint(ep)) return false;
return true;
}
function isFeatherlessEndpoint(ep) {
if (!ep) return false;
if (ep.provider === 'featherless') return true;
const url = String(ep.base_url || '').toLowerCase();
try {
const host = new URL(url).hostname;
return host === 'api.featherless.ai' ||
host.endsWith('.featherless.ai');
} catch (_) {
return url.includes('api.featherless.ai');
}
}

Step 34 — important architectural discovery
The saved endpoint list does not perform a live connection test. It deliberately uses cached
model inventory:
Consequently, its current online value means approximately models are known or the
endpoint is enabled, not the provider has just authenticated successfully.
status = "online" if (all_models or visible or pinned or (is_featherless and
r.is_enabled)) else ("empty" if r.is_enabled else "offline")
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 276/588
You said:
We should preserve that distinction rather than silently turning a cached inventory into a live
health check.
My recommendation is to keep two concepts separate:
Model inventory: cached models and whether the endpoint is enabled.
Provider health: the result and timestamp of an explicit connection test, with ONLINE ,
DEGRADED , OFFLINE , AUTH ERROR or NOT TESTED .
That also avoids adding network calls to the endpoint-list page, which was intentionally
designed to respond immediately.

Step 35 — findings
This confirms an important architectural issue: the saved endpoint list does not report live
connectivity.
The /model-endpoints list route is cache-only. It derives status from the presence of cached
or pinned models:
Cached/pinned models → online
Enabled endpoint without models → empty
Disabled endpoint → offline
So an endpoint can display as Online even when its API key is invalid or the provider is
unreachable. The UI then renders that as a normal model-count badge.
The create/update route has the same limitation: its saved status is also derived from cached
models, not a fresh authenticated health check.
That means we should keep three concepts separate:
1. Model inventory — which models are cached, discovered or pinned.
2. Connection health — whether an authenticated request to the provider succeeds.
3. Provider configuration — whether a credential exists and where it came from.
We should not make the existing model-list route perform network requests just to correct its
badge. That would undermine its deliberate cache-only behavior.
Next: Step 36 — inspect the existing health API
Before designing the change, let's find whether the app already exposes a suitable health result
to the UI.

def _rollup_items(name: str, noun: str, items: List[Dict[str, Any]],
key: str = "accounts") -> Dict[str, Any]:
"""Shared ok/degraded/down rollup for a list of per-item probe
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 302/588
results."""
total = len(items)
ok_count = sum(1 for it in items if it.get("ok"))
if ok_count == total:
status, detail = OK, f"{ok_count}/{total} {noun} reachable."
elif ok_count == 0:
status, detail = DOWN, f"No {noun} reachable."
else:
status, detail = DEGRADED, f"{ok_count}/{total} {noun}
reachable."
return _svc(name, status, detail, **{key: items})
# ── Aggregate ──
def _rollup(services: List[Dict[str, Any]]) -> str:
worst = OK
for s in services:
sev = _SEVERITY.get(s.get("status"))
if sev is not None and sev > _SEVERITY[worst]:
worst = s["status"]
return worst

async def _run_subsystem(name: str, fn: Callable, *args: Any) ->
Dict[str, Any]:
"""Run one (sync) subsystem probe in a thread under a hard
deadline.
A subsystem that overruns _SUBSYSTEM_DEADLINE (or raises)
becomes a
controlled down/timeout entry instead of hanging or leaking the
error.
"""
try:
return await asyncio.wait_for(asyncio.to_thread(fn, *args),
timeout=_SUBSYSTEM_DEADLINE)
except asyncio.TimeoutError:
return _svc(name, DOWN, _detail_for("timeout"),
error="timeout")
except Exception as e:
category = _classify_error(e)
return _svc(name, DOWN, _detail_for(category),
error=category)

"""Run every probe and return {overall, services, timestamp}.
Bounded end-to-end: in-process ChromaDB flags are read
synchronously; the
four network subsystems run concurrently, each under
_SUBSYSTEM_DEADLINE,
with an overall _AGGREGATE_DEADLINE backstop. Per-item
probes inside

names = ["searxng", "ntfy", "email", "providers"]
coros = [
_run_subsystem("searxng", searxng_health, settings),
_run_subsystem("ntfy", ntfy_health, inputs["integrations"],
settings),
_run_subsystem("email", email_health, inputs["accounts"]),
_run_subsystem("providers", providers_health,
inputs["endpoints"]),
]
try:
results = await asyncio.wait_for(asyncio.gather(*coros),
timeout=_AGGREGATE_DEADLINE)
except asyncio.TimeoutError:
# Hard backstop — should not normally fire given persubsystem deadlines.
results = [_svc(n, DOWN, _detail_for("timeout"),
error="timeout")
for n in names]
services = [chroma, *results]
return {
"overall": _rollup(services),
"services": services,

📦[deck@odysseus-box ~/OdysseusProject]$
Step 37 — findings
The existing /api/diagnostics/services route is the only identified consumer of the
consolidated service-health report.
That gives us a useful boundary:
Keep the existing diagnostics report and its general ok / degraded / down / disabled
vocabulary intact.
Do not use its aggregate provider probe as the authoritative result for Groq
authentication.
Build provider-specific connection testing around a shared credential resolver, then expose
the richer provider status separately or as an explicitly distinct field.
Preserve the current bounded, non-intrusive diagnostics behavior. It should not
unexpectedly start testing every cloud provider with credentials from new files.

Step 38 — findings
This confirms an important separation in Odysseus:
1. The consolidated service-health API is not currently displayed in the frontend.
/api/diagnostics/services is registered and admin-protected, but the frontend search
found no caller for it. The only diagnostics frontend reference is /api/diagnostics/logs .
2. Provider health currently probes model discovery, not authentication independently.
providers_health() calls _probe_endpoint() . A non-empty model list is treated as
success; an empty list is treated as failure. That means the aggregate health report can
inherit the discovery and fallback problems we identified earlier.
3. The existing health tests protect that current behaviour.
We will need to update or extend those tests if we change provider-health semantics. We
should preserve the overall diagnostics response shape and the existing health vocabulary
for other services.
Design implication: provider connection status should be handled as a distinct capability,
rather than changing the meaning of the entire diagnostics subsystem. We can expose richer
provider states without disrupting ChromaDB, email, SearXNG or ntfy health reporting.
Step 39 — trace the saved endpoint status
We still need to establish exactly how the endpoint-management screen gets its saved online
and status values. This is separate from the consolidated diagnostics API.

📦[deck@odysseus-box ~/OdysseusProject]$
Step 39 — confirmed: saved endpoint status is not a live health check
The endpoint list route explicitly says it is cache-only. It does not contact Groq or any other
provider when the settings page loads.
Its current status is inferred from the cached model inventory:
The frontend then renders that inferred status as a badge.
This exposes an important issue: an endpoint can appear online because it has cached
models, even if its API key has expired or the provider is currently unreachable. Conversely,
an enabled endpoint with no cached models can appear as “no models” without telling us
whether authentication succeeded.
We should preserve the cache-only behaviour of the list route. Making every settings-page
refresh perform network requests would be undesirable. Instead, the design should distinguish:
Inventory: cached model availability.
Connection health: result of an explicit provider test or a separately scheduled/manual
health check.
status = "online" if (
all_models or visible or pinned
or (is_featherless and r.is_enabled)
) else ("empty" if r.is_enabled else "offline")

OAuth/session credentials.

richer health information is introduced separately.

Step 40 — the first important finding
The explicit model-probe path already has a credential-resolution helper:
That is encouraging: Odysseus already has a shared runtime resolver used by model probing. It
gives us a natural integration point for host-mounted provider keys, rather than inventing
another credential store.
However, the refresh code also contains provider-specific exceptions and fallback behaviour.
We need to inspect those before deciding whether the existing resolver can safely serve all
seven providers.## What Step 40 reveals
There is an inconsistency we should resolve before implementation:
The individual model-list refresh route uses _resolve_probe_key(ep) , so it already goes
through the shared credential resolver.
Other probe paths pass ep.get("api_key") directly to _probe_endpoint() and
_probe_single_model() . Those paths would not automatically benefit from host-file
credentials.
Failed manual refreshes deliberately retain the previous cached model list. That is good
behaviour and should remain unchanged.
Background refresh tracks last_success , last_failure and fail_count in runtime
state, but this is not yet the persistent provider-health record we want.
Source: the Step 40 output you supplied. GitHub +1
def _resolve_probe_key(ep):
from src.endpoint_resolver import resolve_endpoint_runtime
_base, key = resolve_endpoint_runtime(
ep, owner=getattr(ep, "owner", None)
)
return key

def _picker_requires_pinning(base_url: str, kind: str) -> bool:
return _classify_endpoint(base_url, kind) == "api"
def _has_explicit_pinned_models(ep) -> bool:
"""Whether pinned_models was deliberately written for this
endpoint.
API endpoints use pinned_models as an allow-list. An explicit
empty JSON
list means "show no models"; it must not fall back to the old
hidden-list
migration behavior.
"""
raw = getattr(ep, "pinned_models", None)
return raw is not None and str(raw).strip() != ""
def _legacy_visible_api_models(ep) -> List[str]:
"""Return API models selected under the old hidden-list picker.
Before API endpoints switched to an explicit allow-list, selected
models
were represented as cached_models minus hidden_models.
Existing OpenRouter
rows can therefore have many checked models and an empty
pinned_models
field. Treat that old state as the initial pinned list so settings and
chat
agree after upgrade.
"""
return _visible_models(
_cached_model_ids(ep),
getattr(ep, "hidden_models", None),
None,

def _picker_models_for_endpoint(ep, base_url: str, kind: str):
"""Return model IDs that should appear in the picker for an
endpoint.
API providers expose remote inventory from /v1/models. Default
to that
visible inventory until an explicit pinned-model allow-list is saved.
Local/self-hosted endpoints keep the older hide-list behavior.
"""
pinned = _normalize_model_ids(getattr(ep, "pinned_models",
None))
if _picker_requires_pinning(base_url, kind):
if not _has_explicit_pinned_models(ep):
pinned = _legacy_visible_api_models(ep)
return pinned, pinned
return _visible_models(
_cached_model_ids(ep),
getattr(ep, "hidden_models", None),
pinned,
), pinned
def _chatgpt_endpoint_visible(ep: Any, request: Request) -> bool:

def _api_key_fingerprint(api_key: Optional[str]) -> str:
"""Stable, non-secret label for distinguishing same-URL
credentials."""
key = (api_key or "").strip()
if not key:
return ""
return hashlib.sha256(key.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()[:8]

# Per-user cache: { owner_key: {"data": ..., "time": ...} }. owner_key

# Track model-list refreshes by URL+key. This prevents repeated
picker/API
# opens from starting duplicate /models probes, and gives
slow/offline
# providers a cooldown after failures.
_refresh_state: Dict[str, Dict[str, Any]] = {}
_refresh_inflight = {"v": False, "done": None} # coarse singleflight guard
_REFRESH_FAILURE_BASE = 300.0
_REFRESH_FAILURE_MAX = 3600.0
def _refresh_key(base: str, api_key: Optional[str], provider_auth_id:
Optional[str] = None) -> str:
# Session-backed endpoints carry no static key; include their
auth id
# so two accounts on one provider URL never share refresh
state.
return f"{base.rstrip('/')}\x00{api_key or ''}\x00{provider_auth_id
or ''}"
def _ts(value: Any) -> float:
try:
return float(value.timestamp()) if value else 0.0
except Exception:
return 0.0
def _failure_delay(fails: int, *, empty_local: bool = False) -> float:
if fails <= 0:
return 0.0
if empty_local:
return min(5.0 * (2 ** max(0, fails - 1)), 30.0)
return min(_REFRESH_FAILURE_BASE * (2 ** max(0, fails - 1)),
_REFRESH_FAILURE_MAX)
def _should_refresh_endpoint(ep: Any, now: float, force: bool =
False) -> tuple[bool, Dict[str, Any]]:
base = _normalize_base(getattr(ep, "base_url", "") or "")
kind = _effective_endpoint_kind(ep, base)
category = _classify_endpoint(base, kind)
mode = _endpoint_refresh_mode(ep, kind)
cached = _cached_model_ids(ep)

https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 341/588
key = _refresh_key(base, getattr(ep, "api_key", None),
getattr(ep, "provider_auth_id", None))
state = _refresh_state.get(key, {})
info = {
"id": getattr(ep, "id", ""),
"base": base,
"api_key": getattr(ep, "api_key", None),
"kind": kind,
"category": category,
"mode": mode,

if not base:
return False, info
if _host_match(base, "featherless.ai") or
_safe_detect_provider(base) == "featherless":
return False, info
if state.get("inflight"):
return False, info
if mode in ("manual", "disabled") and not force:
return False, info
fails = int(state.get("fail_count") or 0)
if fails and not force:
last_failure = float(state.get("last_failure") or 0.0)
empty_local = (
not cached
and category == "local"
and str(getattr(ep, "id", "") or "").startswith("local-")
)
if now - last_failure < _failure_delay(fails,
empty_local=empty_local):
return False, info
if cached and not force:
interval = _endpoint_refresh_interval(ep, category)
last_good = float(state.get("last_success") or 0.0) or
_ts(getattr(ep, "updated_at", None)) or _ts(getattr(ep, "created_at",
None))
if last_good and now - last_good < interval:
return False, info
return True, info

def _probe_one(key: str, data: Dict[str, Any]):
try:
ids = _probe_endpoint(data["base"],
data.get("api_key"), timeout=data.get("timeout") or 2)
return key, data["endpoint_ids"], ids, None
except Exception as e:
return key, data["endpoint_ids"], None, e
if groups:
with ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=min(4,
len(groups))) as pool:
futures = [pool.submit(_probe_one, key, data) for
key, data in groups.items()]
for fut in as_completed(futures):
key, endpoint_ids, ids, err = fut.result()
st = _refresh_state.setdefault(key, {})
if ids:
for ep_id in endpoint_ids:
ep_obj =
db.query(ModelEndpoint).filter(ModelEndpoint.id == ep_id).first()
if ep_obj:
ep_obj.cached_models =
json.dumps(ids)
changed = True
st["last_success"] = _time.time()
st["fail_count"] = 0
st.pop("last_failure", None)
else:
st["last_failure"] = _time.time()
st["fail_count"] = int(st.get("fail_count") or 0)
+ 1
st["inflight"] = False
db.commit()
finally:
db.close()
if changed:
_invalidate_models_cache()
except Exception as e:
logger.warning('Background endpoint refresh failed: %s',
e)
finally:
for st in _refresh_state.values():
st["inflight"] = False
_refresh_inflight["v"] = False

return done

Step 41 — findings
The background refresh and the main streaming probe already use _resolve_probe_key(ep) .
So the shared resolver is more widely integrated than the earlier call-site review suggested.
There are, however, some remaining direct-key paths:
Endpoint creation and the manual connection-test form accept a key directly.
The probe-selected route can fall back to a key supplied in its request when no saved
endpoint is found.
Some direct probe calls operate on keys already resolved earlier, rather than resolving
credentials themselves.
One detail needs checking: background refresh groups endpoints using the database key and
provider-auth ID, but stores the resolved key for the actual probe. We should verify whether
that grouping could accidentally combine endpoints that use different credentials.

def _safe_detect_provider(base_url: str) -> str:

probing."""

ChatGPT said:
Step 42 — Credential resolution findings
This is an important positive finding: Odysseus already has a shared credential-resolution
entry point.
_resolve_probe_key(ep) delegates to
src.endpoint_resolver.resolve_endpoint_runtime() . That resolver is already used by:
Background model refresh
Manual model refresh
Endpoint probing
Several runtime routes elsewhere in the application
That gives us a much safer integration point than adding Groq-specific key-reading code to
each probe route.
The existing tests also cover static database keys, provider-auth runtime tokens, and resolution
failures. We can extend this pattern while preserving OAuth/session-backed providers and
existing local endpoints.
One detail to keep in view: the background refresh grouping key is currently built from
ep.api_key , not the resolved host-file credential. We'll need to assess that when
implementing the shared resolver, so refresh grouping and caching remain correct.
Additional point from results02.txt
The linked file contains the earlier Step 40 output. It confirms:
Model refresh is deliberately cached-first.
Background refreshes are bounded and do not clear a working cached model list after a
failed probe.
Manual refresh retains cached models when a refresh fails.
Existing tests cover probe-key resolution, but there is little direct coverage of the
background refresh grouping behavior.
This is useful: the Groq integration should not change the existing model-cache policy.
Next — Step 43: inspect the live chat credential paths
One important distinction remains. The shared resolver is used by the probe routes, but earlier
inspection found some chat/session routes reading ep.api_key directly. A host-file key would
not automatically reach those paths.

So simply adding host-file support to the existing probe resolver would make Groq appear
discoverable, but chat, session switching, and compare could still send requests without its key.
There is also an important security consideration: session headers are persisted in session
metadata. We should inspect how those headers are stored and restored before deciding
whether host-file credentials should be copied into persistent session state. Ideally, the session
should retain an endpoint reference and resolve its credential at runtime rather than duplicate
the secret.
Next — Step 44: inspect session header persistence
Your host-file rule is clear: the real Groq key must not be copied into SQLite's
ModelEndpoint.api_key , and we should avoid persisting it in session headers either.
Before designing the change, let's inspect how sessions save and restore headers, and whether
the normal chat request can resolve credentials from endpoint_id at send time.

Step 44 — Findings from results03.txt
Useful architecture confirmed
1. Session headers are persisted and restored
The session manager:
Reads DbSession.headers when loading a session.
Restores those headers into the in-memory session.
Writes headers when creating sessions.
Synchronizes them back to the database.
The chat route also persists headers when reconciling a selected endpoint. GitHub +1
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 392/588
This is a significant security consideration: session headers can contain an Authorization:
Bearer ... value. We should not put host-file provider keys into this persistent field. The exact
database column type and any protection it provides still need verification.
2. The main chat path has an endpoint-aware routing layer
The main chat flow passes the session's endpoint ID into its foreground route-descriptor
builder. That gives us a potential way to resolve credentials using the registered endpoint,
rather than relying on stored session headers. GitHub
3. The common resolver is suitable as the foundation
resolve_endpoint_runtime() already handles:
Static API keys
Session-backed provider credentials
Runtime base URL normalization
build_headers() then constructs provider-specific headers, including Groq's standard Bearer
authorization. GitHub
What I conclude
The desired architecture should be:
Host provider key file
│
▼
Shared credential resolver
│
├── Model discovery / refresh
├── Connection test
├── Main chat request
├── Session switch
├── Compare request
└── Other registered-endpoint calls
│
▼
Temporary request headers
│
▼
Provider API
Session database:
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 393/588
Existing explicit custom-endpoint credentials and OAuth/session-backed credentials must
continue working. This is a target architecture, not a claim that all these paths already support
it.

Step 45 — findings
This confirms an important credential-handling issue:
1. Session headers are stored as ordinary JSON. DbSession.headers is Column(JSON,
default=dict) , not an encrypted credential field. Persisting a Groq bearer token there
would put it in the session database in readable form.
2. The foreground candidate builder does not resolve credentials. It simply places the
supplied headers into the primary request candidate. Therefore, the credential must be
resolved before the chat request is built.
3. Route identity is already handled separately. build_foreground_route_descriptors()
uses selected_endpoint_id to resolve the exact registered endpoint and avoid confusing
two endpoints sharing the same URL. We should preserve this behavior.
4. Existing tests give us a useful foundation. tests/test_foreground_model_routing.py
already checks selected endpoint identity and request routing. We can extend this
coverage rather than redesign foreground routing.
Design consequence: host-file credentials must be resolved at request time and supplied as
temporary request headers. They must not be copied into DbSession.headers . Existing
explicitly configured custom endpoint credentials and OAuth credentials must continue to
work.
Step 46 — inspect the remaining credential boundaries
Before proposing changes, we need to establish exactly where session headers are created,
persisted, and restored, and whether the selected endpoint can be resolved safely at chat time.
ODYSSEUS — odysseus-box (CONTAINER)
This is read-only. It does not access or reveal any API key values.

Step 46 — findings
We have now confirmed the main security boundary, and there is one additional issue worth
addressing before implementation.
1. Session database currently stores credentials
The following paths can persist authorization headers in DbSession.headers :
Session creation — routes/session_routes.py
Session endpoint switching — routes/session_routes.py
Chat route reconciliation — routes/chat_routes.py
Session restoration — core/session_manager.py
That means simply adding host-file credentials to the existing session headers would be unsafe.
2. The foreground router is not the right place to load keys
build_foreground_model_candidates() is deliberately simple: it accepts a URL, model and
headers and constructs candidates. Endpoint identity resolution happens separately.
10/31/26, 2:18 AM *1.1* Odysseus - Groq Step Inspection
https://chatgpt.com/c/6ae4a773-eefc-83ed-8b31-2030acc8ce24 440/588
The shared credential resolver should therefore sit at the endpoint/request boundary, not
inside the candidate builder.
3. There are additional consumers beyond ordinary chat
The output identifies background monitoring, session tools and history compaction as
consumers of session headers. They will need consideration when we stop storing provider
credentials in sessions. Otherwise, ordinary chat might work while background or secondary
operations fail.
4. Recommended session design
Persist only non-secret route metadata:
endpoint_id
endpoint URL
model
non-sensitive request preferences
Resolve credentials when a request is made. Preserve explicit custom endpoint credentials and
OAuth behavior. Avoid putting host-file keys into session memory or database headers.
Discovery status
Core architecture mapped
We have enough information to define the implementation boundaries, but I recommend one
final read-only inspection before making the proposal: identify every place that consumes
sess.headers or session.headers , and distinguish actual provider requests from unrelated
header use.
Step 47 — session header consumers

Step 47 — Session headers: important finding
The output confirms that session headers are not just used by the main chat route. They are
consumed across chat processing, background jobs, research, memory, history, email,
webhooks and agent tools.
That means we must not simply stop storing session headers or clear them globally to protect
the new host-file credentials. Doing so could break existing custom endpoints, OAuth-backed
sessions and unrelated workflows.

You said:
The safer design is to distinguish credentials resolved from a provider key file from legacy or
explicitly supplied session headers:
Resolve host-file credentials when making a request to a registered provider endpoint.
Keep the key out of persisted session headers.
Preserve existing session-header behaviour for other endpoint types.
Avoid changing unrelated consumers unless inspection proves they need to change.
One detail remains unresolved: the actual database column type for DbSession.headers . We
need to establish whether it is plain JSON/text or protected by a custom encrypted type before
deciding how to handle existing sessions.
